========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well… I guess I’m here because things have gotten kind of sideways at home. Ever since my father-in-law moved in, I’ve been snapping at my wife over stupid little house things — dishes, where stuff gets put, that kind of nonsense. And I don’t really like hearing myself do it.

I used to keep on top of jobs around the place and paperwork, bills, forms, whatever, but lately I just keep putting it off. I’ll go out to the workshop thinking I’ll clear my head — I’ve got this old bench I’ve been meaning to fix, actually — or I’ll try to go fishing, because usually that settles me down. But my head just keeps running. It’s like I can’t get quiet inside.

I mean, I still go out to the workshop sometimes, but I don’t really do what I went out there to do. I’ll stand around, move a few tools, then remember there’s paperwork inside I’ve been putting off, then somehow I’m looking at an old fishing reel that needs fixing… and nothing gets done.\n\nI used to stay on top of jobs around the house and bills and forms, that kind of thing. Since my father-in-law moved in, I just keep avoiding it. And even fishing — I still try, because usually that clears my head, but lately I can’t seem to get quiet inside, so it doesn’t feel the same. Mostly I end up snapping at my wife over little house stuff instead of actually dealing with anything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest exactly. I still like the idea of things I used to enjoy, if that makes sense. Sometimes I’ll start something and then sort of drift off or get distracted.

It’s like I can’t get quiet inside.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d say every second of the day, but a lot of the time I feel wound up. Since my father-in-law moved in, it’s like I’m always bracing for something, and then I end up snapping at my wife over dumb little house stuff.\n\nEven when I try to go out to the workshop or take myself fishing — which usually helps, you know, just the quiet and doing something with my hands — my head doesn’t really settle. I keep thinking about the jobs I’m not doing, paperwork piling up, little things around the house. It just doesn’t get quiet in there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or hopeless lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… sad, maybe not exactly. Hopeless sounds a bit strong. I get fed up, I guess. Like when you go to fix one thing in the house and then notice six other things wrong — actually that happened with the shed door the other day, the hinge was sticking and I ended up pulling half the frame apart.\n\nBut, sorry… I’m not sure I’d call it sadness. More like I’m worn down and frustrated.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to do that you're avoiding now?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I suppose so. I mean, I still go out to the workshop sometimes, but I don’t really do what I went out there to do. I’ll stand around, move a few tools, then remember there’s paperwork inside I’ve been putting off, then somehow I’m looking at an old fishing reel that needs fixing… and nothing gets done.\n\nI used to stay on top of jobs around the house and bills and forms, that kind of thing. Since my father-in-law moved in, I just keep avoiding it. And even fishing — I still try, because usually that clears my head, but lately I can’t seem to get quiet inside, so it doesn’t feel the same. Mostly I end up snapping at my wife over little house stuff instead of actually dealing with anything.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain places, people, or activities because you fear something might happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s fear like that, no. I mean, I avoid some things, but it’s more because I don’t want to deal with them or I’m tired of the whole mess, not because I think something bad is going to happen.\n\nLike, I’ll put off going into town for errands, but then I’ll end up in the garage looking for something and find a box of old screws I forgot about—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. No, I haven’t noticed avoiding places or people because I’m scared something might happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you can't get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sort of. Not like a phrase over and over, exactly. It’s more that I can’t get my head to settle. Ever since my father-in-law moved in, it’s like everything in the house has become this thing I keep chewing on.\n\nI’ll be out in the workshop, meaning to fix something simple, and then I’m thinking about paperwork I haven’t done, or some little thing my wife said, or I end up snapping at her about, I don’t know, a cupboard door or something stupid. Even fishing doesn’t quiet it down like it used to. So yes, I guess the same worries keep circling.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing things over and over, like checking or cleaning, that you don't really want to be doing?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… not like checking the stove ten times or cleaning the same spot or anything like that.\n\nI mean, I’ll pick things up and put them down in the garage more than I need to, but that’s more me getting sidetracked than feeling like I have to do it. I don’t think there’s some routine I’m stuck repeating. Unless you mean something different by it?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest exactly. I still like the idea of things I used to enjoy, if that makes sense. Sometimes I’ll start something and then sort of drift off or get distracted.\n\nLike, I was going to sort through some fishing gear the other day, and then I ended up spending half an hour looking for a little plastic container I’m pretty sure I threw out two years ago. So… I don’t know. Maybe I’m not getting into things the same way, but I wouldn’t say everything feels completely uninteresting.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.0, F48.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I’m here because things have gotten kind of sideways at home. Ever since my father-in-law moved in, I’ve been snapping at my wife over stupid little house things — dishes, where stuff gets put, that kind of nonsense. And I don’t really like hearing myself do it.\n\nI used to keep on top of jobs around the place and paperwork, bills, forms, whatever, but lately I just keep putting it off. I’ll go out to the workshop thinking I’ll clear my head — I’ve got this old bench I’ve been meaning to fix, actually — or I’ll try to go fishing, because usually that settles me down. But my head just keeps running. It’s like I can’t get quiet inside.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d say every second of the day, but a lot of the time I feel wound up. Since my father-in-law moved in, it’s like I’m always bracing for something, and then I end up snapping at my wife over dumb little house stuff.\n\nEven when I try to go out to the workshop or take myself fishing — which usually helps, you know, just the quiet and doing something with my hands — my head doesn’t really settle. I keep thinking about the jobs I’m not doing, paperwork piling up, little things around the house. It just doesn’t get quiet in there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or hopeless lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… sad, maybe not exactly. Hopeless sounds a bit strong. I get fed up, I guess. Like when you go to fix one thing in the house and then notice six other things wrong — actually that happened with the shed door the other day, the hinge was sticking and I ended up pulling half the frame apart.\n\nBut, sorry… I’m not sure I’d call it sadness. More like I’m worn down and frustrated.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to do that you're avoiding now?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I suppose so. I mean, I still go out to the workshop sometimes, but I don’t really do what I went out there to do. I’ll stand around, move a few tools, then remember there’s paperwork inside I’ve been putting off, then somehow I’m looking at an old fishing reel that needs fixing… and nothing gets done.\n\nI used to stay on top of jobs around the house and bills and forms, that kind of thing. Since my father-in-law moved in, I just keep avoiding it. And even fishing — I still try, because usually that clears my head, but lately I can’t seem to get quiet inside, so it doesn’t feel the same. Mostly I end up snapping at my wife over little house stuff instead of actually dealing with anything.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain places, people, or activities because you fear something might happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s fear like that, no. I mean, I avoid some things, but it’s more because I don’t want to deal with them or I’m tired of the whole mess, not because I think something bad is going to happen.\n\nLike, I’ll put off going into town for errands, but then I’ll end up in the garage looking for something and find a box of old screws I forgot about—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. No, I haven’t noticed avoiding places or people because I’m scared something might happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you can't get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sort of. Not like a phrase over and over, exactly. It’s more that I can’t get my head to settle. Ever since my father-in-law moved in, it’s like everything in the house has become this thing I keep chewing on.\n\nI’ll be out in the workshop, meaning to fix something simple, and then I’m thinking about paperwork I haven’t done, or some little thing my wife said, or I end up snapping at her about, I don’t know, a cupboard door or something stupid. Even fishing doesn’t quiet it down like it used to. So yes, I guess the same worries keep circling.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing things over and over, like checking or cleaning, that you don't really want to be doing?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… not like checking the stove ten times or cleaning the same spot or anything like that.\n\nI mean, I’ll pick things up and put them down in the garage more than I need to, but that’s more me getting sidetracked than feeling like I have to do it. I don’t think there’s some routine I’m stuck repeating. Unless you mean something different by it?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest exactly. I still like the idea of things I used to enjoy, if that makes sense. Sometimes I’ll start something and then sort of drift off or get distracted.\n\nLike, I was going to sort through some fishing gear the other day, and then I ended up spending half an hour looking for a little plastic container I’m pretty sure I threw out two years ago. So… I don’t know. Maybe I’m not getting into things the same way, but I wouldn’t say everything feels completely uninteresting.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I haven’t been weighing myself or anything. My belt feels about the same, I guess.\n\nMy wife’s the one who’d probably notice before I would—she notices if I leave a coffee mug in the wrong place, so she’d definitely notice that. But no, nothing that’s jumped out at me as a big change.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced or witnessed any traumatic events recently?\n\nPatient: Not that I can think of, no. I mean, nothing like… I don’t know, an accident or violence or something like that.\n\nI did see a pretty bad wreck once years ago, but that’s not recent, and honestly I’m not sure why my brain went there. Lately it’s more just regular life stuff, I guess. Nothing I’d call traumatic recently.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Sometimes I’ll start something and then sort of drift off or get distracted.\n\nLike, I was going to sort through some fishing gear the other day, and then I ended up spending half an hour looking for a little plastic container I’m pretty sure I threw out two years ago. So… I don’t know. Maybe I’m not getting into things the same way, but I wouldn’t say everything feels completely uninteresting.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I haven’t been weighing myself or anything. My belt feels about the same, I guess.\n\nMy wife’s the one who’d probably notice before I would—she notices if I leave a coffee mug in the wrong place, so she’d definitely notice that. But no, nothing that’s jumped out at me as a big change.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced or witnessed any traumatic events recently?\n\nPatient: Not that I can think of, no. I mean, nothing like… I don’t know, an accident or violence or something like that.\n\nI did see a pretty bad wreck once years ago, but that’s not recent, and honestly I’m not sure why my brain went there. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.     "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think I probably am, in a way. Not like I’m hiding in the garage every time he walks in, but since he moved in, everything feels… crowded, I guess.

I’ll put off dealing with stuff around the house, paperwork, little jobs I used to just knock out. And then I end up snapping at my wife over stupid things, like where something got put or how the kitchen is arranged. The other day I got irritated about a stack of mail like it was some major crisis, which is ridiculous.

Even when I go out to the workshop or try to fish—things that usually settle me down—I can’t really get my head quiet. I start thinking about the house and him being there and what needs to be dealt with, and then I’m off on some whole other track. So… yeah, I guess I’m avoiding it some, or at least not handling it very well.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure… I don’t think I’d say distant exactly. I still care about my wife and what’s going on, and things do still get to me.

Sometimes I probably check out a little, like I’ll be sitting there and she’s talking and my mind wanders off to, I don’t know, whether the gutters need cleaning or something totally unrelated. My dad used to do that too—he’d stare right past you during dinner, and then suddenly ask if the lawn mower had gas in it. Drove my mom nuts.

But emotionally distant… I don’t know. Maybe not in the way you mean. I haven’t really noticed that I feel numb or anything like that.

I’ll go out to the workshop thinking I’ll clear my head — I’ve got this old bench I’ve been meaning to fix, actually — or I’ll try to go fishing, because usually that settles me down. But my head just keeps running. It’s like I can’t get quiet inside.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d say every second of the day, but a lot of the time I feel wound up. Since my father-in-law moved in, it’s like I’m always bracing for something, and then I end up snapping at my wife over dumb little house stuff.\n\nEven when I try to go out to the workshop or take myself fishing — which usually helps, you know, just the quiet and doing something with my hands — my head doesn’t really settle. I keep thinking about the jobs I’m not doing, paperwork piling up, little things around the house. It just doesn’t get quiet in there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or hopeless lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… sad, maybe not exactly. Hopeless sounds a bit strong. I get fed up, I guess. Like when you go to fix one thing in the house and then notice six other things wrong — actually that happened with the shed door the other day, the hinge was sticking and I ended up pulling half the frame apart.\n\nBut, sorry… I’m not sure I’d call it sadness. More like I’m worn down and frustrated.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to do that you're avoiding now?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I suppose so. I mean, I still go out to the workshop sometimes, but I don’t really do what I went out there to do. I’ll stand around, move a few tools, then remember there’s paperwork inside I’ve been putting off, then somehow I’m looking at an old fishing reel that needs fixing… and nothing gets done.\n\nI used to stay on top of jobs around the house and bills and forms, that kind of thing. Since my father-in-law moved in, I just keep avoiding it. And even fishing — I still try, because usually that clears my head, but lately I can’t seem to get quiet inside, so it doesn’t feel the same. Mostly I end up snapping at my wife over little house stuff instead of actually dealing with anything.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain places, people, or activities because you fear something might happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s fear like that, no. I mean, I avoid some things, but it’s more because I don’t want to deal with them or I’m tired of the whole mess, not because I think something bad is going to happen.\n\nLike, I’ll put off going into town for errands, but then I’ll end up in the garage looking for something and find a box of old screws I forgot about—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. No, I haven’t noticed avoiding places or people because I’m scared something might happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you can't get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sort of. Not like a phrase over and over, exactly. It’s more that I can’t get my head to settle. Ever since my father-in-law moved in, it’s like everything in the house has become this thing I keep chewing on.\n\nI’ll be out in the workshop, meaning to fix something simple, and then I’m thinking about paperwork I haven’t done, or some little thing my wife said, or I end up snapping at her about, I don’t know, a cupboard door or something stupid. Even fishing doesn’t quiet it down like it used to. So yes, I guess the same worries keep circling.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing things over and over, like checking or cleaning, that you don't really want to be doing?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… not like checking the stove ten times or cleaning the same spot or anything like that.\n\nI mean, I’ll pick things up and put them down in the garage more than I need to, but that’s more me getting sidetracked than feeling like I have to do it. I don’t think there’s some routine I’m stuck repeating. Unless you mean something different by it?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest exactly. I still like the idea of things I used to enjoy, if that makes sense. Sometimes I’ll start something and then sort of drift off or get distracted.\n\nLike, I was going to sort through some fishing gear the other day, and then I ended up spending half an hour looking for a little plastic container I’m pretty sure I threw out two years ago. So… I don’t know. Maybe I’m not getting into things the same way, but I wouldn’t say everything feels completely uninteresting.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I haven’t been weighing myself or anything. My belt feels about the same, I guess.\n\nMy wife’s the one who’d probably notice before I would—she notices if I leave a coffee mug in the wrong place, so she’d definitely notice that. But no, nothing that’s jumped out at me as a big change.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced or witnessed any traumatic events recently?\n\nPatient: Not that I can think of, no. I mean, nothing like… I don’t know, an accident or violence or something like that.\n\nI did see a pretty bad wreck once years ago, but that’s not recent, and honestly I’m not sure why my brain went there. Lately it’s more just regular life stuff, I guess. Nothing I’d call traumatic recently.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're avoiding dealing with your father-in-law or the situation with him living with you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably am, in a way. I haven’t really noticed that I feel numb or anything like that.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often feel guilty about things you've said or done, especially towards your wife?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 47 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because ever since my father-in-law moved in, I’ve been snapping at my wife over little house things, putting off jobs and paperwork I used to stay on top of, and I just can’t seem to get my head quiet even when I’m out in the workshop or trying to fish.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do feel bad about it, especially with my wife. I mean, I’m here because ever since my father-in-law moved in, I’ve been snapping at her over these tiny house things that shouldn’t matter that much.

Like, I’ll get worked up about some job I haven’t done or paperwork sitting there, and instead of just dealing with it, I put it off and then somehow she catches the edge of it. It’s stupid. I used to be better at staying on top of that kind of stuff.

And even when I try to get away from it—go out to the workshop or go fishing, which used to clear my head—I can’t really settle down. My mind just keeps chewing on the house situation, and then I come back already irritated. So yeah, I feel guilty. She doesn’t deserve me taking it out on her.

I don’t think there’s some routine I’m stuck repeating. So… I don’t know. Maybe I’m not getting into things the same way, but I wouldn’t say everything feels completely uninteresting.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I haven’t been weighing myself or anything. And then I end up snapping at my wife over stupid things, like where something got put or how the kitchen is arranged. The other day I got irritated about a stack of mail like it was some major crisis, which is ridiculous.\n\nEven when I go out to the workshop or try to fish—things that usually settle me down—I can’t really get my head quiet. I still care about my wife and what’s going on, and things do still get to me.\n\nSometimes I probably check out a little, like I’ll be sitting there and she’s talking and my mind wanders off to, I don’t know, whether the gutters need cleaning or something totally unrelated. I haven’t really noticed that I feel numb or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things you've said or done, especially towards your wife?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do feel bad about it, especially with my wife. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I guess I do. It’s mostly tied up with the house, though. Ever since my father-in-law moved in, I’m always thinking ahead to what’s going to become a problem next.

Like, there’s some paperwork sitting there, or a small repair I’d usually just handle, and instead I put it off and then it turns into this thing in my head. And then I’m short with my wife over something dumb, like where something got put.

Even when I go out to the workshop or try to fish, my mind doesn’t really settle. I’ll be standing there with a rod in my hand thinking about some little house issue like it’s the end of the world. So yeah… I’m anticipating problems, probably more than I should.

My dad used to do that too—he’d stare right past you during dinner, and then suddenly ask if the lawn mower had gas in it. Drove my mom nuts.\n\nBut emotionally distant… I don’t know. Maybe not in the way you mean. I haven’t really noticed that I feel numb or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things you've said or done, especially towards your wife?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do feel bad about it, especially with my wife. I mean, I’m here because ever since my father-in-law moved in, I’ve been snapping at her over these tiny house things that shouldn’t matter that much.\n\nLike, I’ll get worked up about some job I haven’t done or paperwork sitting there, and instead of just dealing with it, I put it off and then somehow she catches the edge of it. It’s stupid. I used to be better at staying on top of that kind of stuff.\n\nAnd even when I try to get away from it—go out to the workshop or go fishing, which used to clear my head—I can’t really settle down. My mind just keeps chewing on the house situation, and then I come back already irritated. So yeah, I feel guilty. She doesn’t deserve me taking it out on her.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about the future or anticipating potential problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I do. It’s mostly tied up with the house, though. Ever since my father-in-law moved in, I’m always thinking ahead to what’s going to become a problem next.\n\nLike, there’s some paperwork sitting there, or a small repair I’d usually just handle, and instead I put it off and then it turns into this thing in my head. And then I’m short with my wife over something dumb, like where something got put.\n\nEven when I go out to the workshop or try to fish, my mind doesn’t really settle. I’ll be standing there with a rod in my hand thinking about some little house issue like it’s the end of the world. So yeah… I’m anticipating problems, probably more than I should.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like your father-in-law moving in has significantly affected your emotional state?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I think it has. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like it’s all his fault or anything, because he’s just… he needs a place, you know. But since he moved in, I’ve felt more on edge.

I’m here because I keep snapping at my wife over little house stuff, things that shouldn’t turn into arguments. And I’ve been putting off jobs and paperwork I used to just stay on top of. It’s like everything around the house feels bigger than it is.

Even when I go out to the workshop or try to fish, which used to be my way of settling down, my head just won’t quiet. I’ll be thinking about some dumb chore or something I haven’t handled. So yeah… emotionally, it’s definitely affected me.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I’m here because things have gotten kind of sideways at home. Ever since my father-in-law moved in, I’ve been snapping at my wife over stupid little house things — dishes, where stuff gets put, that kind of nonsense. And I don’t really like hearing myself do it.\n\nI used to keep on top of jobs around the place and paperwork, bills, forms, whatever, but lately I just keep putting it off. I’ll go out to the workshop thinking I’ll clear my head — I’ve got this old bench I’ve been meaning to fix, actually — or I’ll try to go fishing, because usually that settles me down. But my head just keeps running. It’s like I can’t get quiet inside.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d say every second of the day, but a lot of the time I feel wound up. Since my father-in-law moved in, it’s like I’m always bracing for something, and then I end up snapping at my wife over dumb little house stuff.\n\nEven when I try to go out to the workshop or take myself fishing — which usually helps, you know, just the quiet and doing something with my hands — my head doesn’t really settle. I keep thinking about the jobs I’m not doing, paperwork piling up, little things around the house. It just doesn’t get quiet in there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or hopeless lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… sad, maybe not exactly. Hopeless sounds a bit strong. I get fed up, I guess. Like when you go to fix one thing in the house and then notice six other things wrong — actually that happened with the shed door the other day, the hinge was sticking and I ended up pulling half the frame apart.\n\nBut, sorry… I’m not sure I’d call it sadness. More like I’m worn down and frustrated.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to do that you're avoiding now?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I suppose so. I mean, I still go out to the workshop sometimes, but I don’t really do what I went out there to do. I’ll stand around, move a few tools, then remember there’s paperwork inside I’ve been putting off, then somehow I’m looking at an old fishing reel that needs fixing… and nothing gets done.\n\nI used to stay on top of jobs around the house and bills and forms, that kind of thing. Since my father-in-law moved in, I just keep avoiding it. And even fishing — I still try, because usually that clears my head, but lately I can’t seem to get quiet inside, so it doesn’t feel the same. Mostly I end up snapping at my wife over little house stuff instead of actually dealing with anything.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain places, people, or activities because you fear something might happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s fear like that, no. I mean, I avoid some things, but it’s more because I don’t want to deal with them or I’m tired of the whole mess, not because I think something bad is going to happen.\n\nLike, I’ll put off going into town for errands, but then I’ll end up in the garage looking for something and find a box of old screws I forgot about—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. No, I haven’t noticed avoiding places or people because I’m scared something might happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you can't get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sort of. Not like a phrase over and over, exactly. It’s more that I can’t get my head to settle. Ever since my father-in-law moved in, it’s like everything in the house has become this thing I keep chewing on.\n\nI’ll be out in the workshop, meaning to fix something simple, and then I’m thinking about paperwork I haven’t done, or some little thing my wife said, or I end up snapping at her about, I don’t know, a cupboard door or something stupid. Even fishing doesn’t quiet it down like it used to. So yes, I guess the same worries keep circling.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing things over and over, like checking or cleaning, that you don't really want to be doing?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… not like checking the stove ten times or cleaning the same spot or anything like that.\n\nI mean, I’ll pick things up and put them down in the garage more than I need to, but that’s more me getting sidetracked than feeling like I have to do it. I don’t think there’s some routine I’m stuck repeating. Unless you mean something different by it?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest exactly. I still like the idea of things I used to enjoy, if that makes sense. Sometimes I’ll start something and then sort of drift off or get distracted.\n\nLike, I was going to sort through some fishing gear the other day, and then I ended up spending half an hour looking for a little plastic container I’m pretty sure I threw out two years ago. So… I don’t know. Maybe I’m not getting into things the same way, but I wouldn’t say everything feels completely uninteresting.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I haven’t been weighing myself or anything. My belt feels about the same, I guess.\n\nMy wife’s the one who’d probably notice before I would—she notices if I leave a coffee mug in the wrong place, so she’d definitely notice that. But no, nothing that’s jumped out at me as a big change.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced or witnessed any traumatic events recently?\n\nPatient: Not that I can think of, no. I mean, nothing like… I don’t know, an accident or violence or something like that.\n\nI did see a pretty bad wreck once years ago, but that’s not recent, and honestly I’m not sure why my brain went there. Lately it’s more just regular life stuff, I guess. Nothing I’d call traumatic recently.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're avoiding dealing with your father-in-law or the situation with him living with you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably am, in a way. Not like I’m hiding in the garage every time he walks in, but since he moved in, everything feels… crowded, I guess.\n\nI’ll put off dealing with stuff around the house, paperwork, little jobs I used to just knock out. And then I end up snapping at my wife over stupid things, like where something got put or how the kitchen is arranged. The other day I got irritated about a stack of mail like it was some major crisis, which is ridiculous.\n\nEven when I go out to the workshop or try to fish—things that usually settle me down—I can’t really get my head quiet. I start thinking about the house and him being there and what needs to be dealt with, and then I’m off on some whole other track. So… yeah, I guess I’m avoiding it some, or at least not handling it very well.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're emotionally distant from things or people that normally would affect you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I’d say distant exactly. I still care about my wife and what’s going on, and things do still get to me.\n\nSometimes I probably check out a little, like I’ll be sitting there and she’s talking and my mind wanders off to, I don’t know, whether the gutters need cleaning or something totally unrelated. She doesn’t deserve me taking it out on her.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about the future or anticipating potential problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I do. It’s mostly tied up with the house, though. Ever since my father-in-law moved in, I’m always thinking ahead to what’s going to become a problem next.\n\nLike, there’s some paperwork sitting there, or a small repair I’d usually just handle, and instead I put it off and then it turns into this thing in my head. And then I’m short with my wife over something dumb, like where something got put.\n\nEven when I go out to the workshop or try to fish, my mind doesn’t really settle. More like I’m worn down and frustrated.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to do that you're avoiding now?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I suppose so. I mean, I still go out to the workshop sometimes, but I don’t really do what I went out there to do. I’ll stand around, move a few tools, then remember there’s paperwork inside I’ve been putting off, then somehow I’m looking at an old fishing reel that needs fixing… and nothing gets done.\n\nI used to stay on top of jobs around the house and bills and forms, that kind of thing. Since my father-in-law moved in, I just keep avoiding it. And even fishing — I still try, because usually that clears my head, but lately I can’t seem to get quiet inside, so it doesn’t feel the same. Mostly I end up snapping at my wife over little house stuff instead of actually dealing with anything.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain places, people, or activities because you fear something might happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s fear like that, no. I mean, I avoid some things, but it’s more because I don’t want to deal with them or I’m tired of the whole mess, not because I think something bad is going to happen.\n\nLike, I’ll put off going into town for errands, but then I’ll end up in the garage looking for something and find a box of old screws I forgot about—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. No, I haven’t noticed avoiding places or people because I’m scared something might happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you can't get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sort of. Not like a phrase over and over, exactly. It’s more that I can’t get my head to settle. Ever since my father-in-law moved in, it’s like everything in the house has become this thing I keep chewing on.\n\nI’ll be out in the workshop, meaning to fix something simple, and then I’m thinking about paperwork I haven’t done, or some little thing my wife said, or I end up snapping at her about, I don’t know, a cupboard door or something stupid. Even fishing doesn’t quiet it down like it used to. So yes, I guess the same worries keep circling.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing things over and over, like checking or cleaning, that you don't really want to be doing?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… not like checking the stove ten times or cleaning the same spot or anything like that.\n\nI mean, I’ll pick things up and put them down in the garage more than I need to, but that’s more me getting sidetracked than feeling like I have to do it. I don’t think there’s some routine I’m stuck repeating. Unless you mean something different by it?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest exactly. I still like the idea of things I used to enjoy, if that makes sense. Sometimes I’ll start something and then sort of drift off or get distracted.\n\nLike, I was going to sort through some fishing gear the other day, and then I ended up spending half an hour looking for a little plastic container I’m pretty sure I threw out two years ago. So… I don’t know. Maybe I’m not getting into things the same way, but I wouldn’t say everything feels completely uninteresting.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I haven’t been weighing myself or anything. My belt feels about the same, I guess.\n\nMy wife’s the one who’d probably notice before I would—she notices if I leave a coffee mug in the wrong place, so she’d definitely notice that. But no, nothing that’s jumped out at me as a big change.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced or witnessed any traumatic events recently?\n\nPatient: Not that I can think of, no. I mean, nothing like… I don’t know, an accident or violence or something like that.\n\nI did see a pretty bad wreck once years ago, but that’s not recent, and honestly I’m not sure why my brain went there. Lately it’s more just regular life stuff, I guess. Nothing I’d call traumatic recently.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're avoiding dealing with your father-in-law or the situation with him living with you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably am, in a way. Not like I’m hiding in the garage every time he walks in, but since he moved in, everything feels… crowded, I guess.\n\nI’ll put off dealing with stuff around the house, paperwork, little jobs I used to just knock out. And then I end up snapping at my wife over stupid things, like where something got put or how the kitchen is arranged. The other day I got irritated about a stack of mail like it was some major crisis, which is ridiculous.\n\nEven when I go out to the workshop or try to fish—things that usually settle me down—I can’t really get my head quiet. I start thinking about the house and him being there and what needs to be dealt with, and then I’m off on some whole other track. So… yeah, I guess I’m avoiding it some, or at least not handling it very well.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're emotionally distant from things or people that normally would affect you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I’d say distant exactly. I still care about my wife and what’s going on, and things do still get to me.\n\nSometimes I probably check out a little, like I’ll be sitting there and she’s talking and my mind wanders off to, I don’t know, whether the gutters need cleaning or something totally unrelated. My dad used to do that too—he’d stare right past you during dinner, and then suddenly ask if the lawn mower had gas in it. Drove my mom nuts.\n\nBut emotionally distant… I don’t know. Maybe not in the way you mean. I haven’t really noticed that I feel numb or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things you've said or done, especially towards your wife?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do feel bad about it, especially with my wife. I mean, I’m here because ever since my father-in-law moved in, I’ve been snapping at her over these tiny house things that shouldn’t matter that much.\n\nLike, I’ll get worked up about some job I haven’t done or paperwork sitting there, and instead of just dealing with it, I put it off and then somehow she catches the edge of it. It’s stupid. I used to be better at staying on top of that kind of stuff.\n\nAnd even when I try to get away from it—go out to the workshop or go fishing, which used to clear my head—I can’t really settle down. My mind just keeps chewing on the house situation, and then I come back already irritated. So yeah, I feel guilty. She doesn’t deserve me taking it out on her.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about the future or anticipating potential problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I do. It’s mostly tied up with the house, though. Ever since my father-in-law moved in, I’m always thinking ahead to what’s going to become a problem next.\n\nLike, there’s some paperwork sitting there, or a small repair I’d usually just handle, and instead I put it off and then it turns into this thing in my head. And then I’m short with my wife over something dumb, like where something got put.\n\nEven when I go out to the workshop or try to fish, my mind doesn’t really settle. I’ll be standing there with a rod in my hand thinking about some little house issue like it’s the end of the world. So yeah… I’m anticipating problems, probably more than I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your father-in-law moving in has significantly affected your emotional state?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it has. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like it’s all his fault or anything, because he’s just… he needs a place, you know. But since he moved in, I’ve felt more on edge.\n\nI’m here because I keep snapping at my wife over little house stuff, things that shouldn’t turn into arguments. And I’ve been putting off jobs and paperwork I used to just stay on top of. It’s like everything around the house feels bigger than it is.\n\nEven when I go out to the workshop or try to fish, which used to be my way of settling down, my head just won’t quiet. I’ll be thinking about some dumb chore or something I haven’t handled. So yeah… emotionally, it’s definitely affected me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat certain behaviors or rituals to feel okay or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like rituals, no. I mean, I’ll sometimes go back and check if I locked the shed or if I left a tool out, but that’s more because I’ve done that before and then the rain gets in—actually I lost a decent set of clamps that way once, which still annoys me.\n\nBut as far as having to repeat something to keep something bad from happening… no, I don’t think that’s really me. I just get stuck thinking about things more than I should, but not in a ritual kind of way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Not really… I mean, I like things done a certain way around the house, I guess, but I don’t know that it’s some specific order I have to follow to feel okay.

Like, if I’m in the workshop, I’ll put tools back where they go, because otherwise I spend twenty minutes looking for a wrench I swear I just had. So… no, I don’t think I’m doing things in a special order to feel in control. I just get bothered when things feel out of place, I guess.

Ever since my father-in-law moved in, I’ve been snapping at my wife over stupid little house things — dishes, where stuff gets put, that kind of nonsense. And I don’t really like hearing myself do it.\n\nI used to keep on top of jobs around the place and paperwork, bills, forms, whatever, but lately I just keep putting it off. I’ll go out to the workshop thinking I’ll clear my head — I’ve got this old bench I’ve been meaning to fix, actually — or I’ll try to go fishing, because usually that settles me down. But my head just keeps running. It’s like I can’t get quiet inside.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d say every second of the day, but a lot of the time I feel wound up. Since my father-in-law moved in, it’s like I’m always bracing for something, and then I end up snapping at my wife over dumb little house stuff.\n\nEven when I try to go out to the workshop or take myself fishing — which usually helps, you know, just the quiet and doing something with my hands — my head doesn’t really settle. I keep thinking about the jobs I’m not doing, paperwork piling up, little things around the house. It just doesn’t get quiet in there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or hopeless lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… sad, maybe not exactly. Hopeless sounds a bit strong. I get fed up, I guess. Like when you go to fix one thing in the house and then notice six other things wrong — actually that happened with the shed door the other day, the hinge was sticking and I ended up pulling half the frame apart.\n\nBut, sorry… I’m not sure I’d call it sadness. More like I’m worn down and frustrated.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to do that you're avoiding now?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I suppose so. I mean, I still go out to the workshop sometimes, but I don’t really do what I went out there to do. I’ll stand around, move a few tools, then remember there’s paperwork inside I’ve been putting off, then somehow I’m looking at an old fishing reel that needs fixing… and nothing gets done.\n\nI used to stay on top of jobs around the house and bills and forms, that kind of thing. Since my father-in-law moved in, I just keep avoiding it. And even fishing — I still try, because usually that clears my head, but lately I can’t seem to get quiet inside, so it doesn’t feel the same. Mostly I end up snapping at my wife over little house stuff instead of actually dealing with anything.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain places, people, or activities because you fear something might happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s fear like that, no. I mean, I avoid some things, but it’s more because I don’t want to deal with them or I’m tired of the whole mess, not because I think something bad is going to happen.\n\nLike, I’ll put off going into town for errands, but then I’ll end up in the garage looking for something and find a box of old screws I forgot about—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. No, I haven’t noticed avoiding places or people because I’m scared something might happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you can't get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sort of. Not like a phrase over and over, exactly. It’s more that I can’t get my head to settle. Ever since my father-in-law moved in, it’s like everything in the house has become this thing I keep chewing on.\n\nI’ll be out in the workshop, meaning to fix something simple, and then I’m thinking about paperwork I haven’t done, or some little thing my wife said, or I end up snapping at her about, I don’t know, a cupboard door or something stupid. Even fishing doesn’t quiet it down like it used to. So yes, I guess the same worries keep circling.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing things over and over, like checking or cleaning, that you don't really want to be doing?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… not like checking the stove ten times or cleaning the same spot or anything like that.\n\nI mean, I’ll pick things up and put them down in the garage more than I need to, but that’s more me getting sidetracked than feeling like I have to do it. I don’t think there’s some routine I’m stuck repeating. Unless you mean something different by it?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest exactly. I still like the idea of things I used to enjoy, if that makes sense. Sometimes I’ll start something and then sort of drift off or get distracted.\n\nLike, I was going to sort through some fishing gear the other day, and then I ended up spending half an hour looking for a little plastic container I’m pretty sure I threw out two years ago. So… I don’t know. Maybe I’m not getting into things the same way, but I wouldn’t say everything feels completely uninteresting.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I haven’t been weighing myself or anything. My belt feels about the same, I guess.\n\nMy wife’s the one who’d probably notice before I would—she notices if I leave a coffee mug in the wrong place, so she’d definitely notice that. But no, nothing that’s jumped out at me as a big change.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced or witnessed any traumatic events recently?\n\nPatient: Not that I can think of, no. I mean, nothing like… I don’t know, an accident or violence or something like that.\n\nI did see a pretty bad wreck once years ago, but that’s not recent, and honestly I’m not sure why my brain went there. Lately it’s more just regular life stuff, I guess. Nothing I’d call traumatic recently.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're avoiding dealing with your father-in-law or the situation with him living with you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably am, in a way. Not like I’m hiding in the garage every time he walks in, but since he moved in, everything feels… crowded, I guess.\n\nI’ll put off dealing with stuff around the house, paperwork, little jobs I used to just knock out. And then I end up snapping at my wife over stupid things, like where something got put or how the kitchen is arranged. The other day I got irritated about a stack of mail like it was some major crisis, which is ridiculous.\n\nEven when I go out to the workshop or try to fish—things that usually settle me down—I can’t really get my head quiet. I start thinking about the house and him being there and what needs to be dealt with, and then I’m off on some whole other track. So… yeah, I guess I’m avoiding it some, or at least not handling it very well.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're emotionally distant from things or people that normally would affect you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I’d say distant exactly. I still care about my wife and what’s going on, and things do still get to me.\n\nSometimes I probably check out a little, like I’ll be sitting there and she’s talking and my mind wanders off to, I don’t know, whether the gutters need cleaning or something totally unrelated. My dad used to do that too—he’d stare right past you during dinner, and then suddenly ask if the lawn mower had gas in it. Drove my mom nuts.\n\nBut emotionally distant… I don’t know. Maybe not in the way you mean. I haven’t really noticed that I feel numb or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things you've said or done, especially towards your wife?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do feel bad about it, especially with my wife. I mean, I’m here because ever since my father-in-law moved in, I’ve been snapping at her over these tiny house things that shouldn’t matter that much.\n\nLike, I’ll get worked up about some job I haven’t done or paperwork sitting there, and instead of just dealing with it, I put it off and then somehow she catches the edge of it. It’s stupid. I used to be better at staying on top of that kind of stuff.\n\nAnd even when I try to get away from it—go out to the workshop or go fishing, which used to clear my head—I can’t really settle down. My mind just keeps chewing on the house situation, and then I come back already irritated. So yeah, I feel guilty. She doesn’t deserve me taking it out on her.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about the future or anticipating potential problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I do. It’s mostly tied up with the house, though. Ever since my father-in-law moved in, I’m always thinking ahead to what’s going to become a problem next.\n\nLike, there’s some paperwork sitting there, or a small repair I’d usually just handle, and instead I put it off and then it turns into this thing in my head. And then I’m short with my wife over something dumb, like where something got put.\n\nEven when I go out to the workshop or try to fish, my mind doesn’t really settle. I’ll be standing there with a rod in my hand thinking about some little house issue like it’s the end of the world. So yeah… I’m anticipating problems, probably more than I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your father-in-law moving in has significantly affected your emotional state?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it has. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like it’s all his fault or anything, because he’s just… he needs a place, you know. But since he moved in, I’ve felt more on edge.\n\nI’m here because I keep snapping at my wife over little house stuff, things that shouldn’t turn into arguments. And I’ve been putting off jobs and paperwork I used to just stay on top of. It’s like everything around the house feels bigger than it is.\n\nEven when I go out to the workshop or try to fish, which used to be my way of settling down, my head just won’t quiet. I’ll be thinking about some dumb chore or something I haven’t handled. So yeah… emotionally, it’s definitely affected me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat certain behaviors or rituals to feel okay or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like rituals, no. I mean, I’ll sometimes go back and check if I locked the shed or if I left a tool out, but that’s more because I’ve done that before and then the rain gets in—actually I lost a decent set of clamps that way once, which still annoys me.\n\nBut as far as having to repeat something to keep something bad from happening… no, I don’t think that’s really me. I just get stuck thinking about things more than I should, but not in a ritual kind of way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing things in a specific order or way to feel more in control?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I like things done a certain way around the house, I guess, but I don’t know that it’s some specific order I have to follow to feel okay.\n\nLike, if I’m in the workshop, I’ll put tools back where they go, because otherwise I spend twenty minutes looking for a wrench I swear I just had. My father-in-law moved some stuff once—not even in a bad way, he was trying to help—and I got more irritated than I probably should’ve. But that’s more about wanting things where I can find them, not like I’m following a ritual.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’m doing things in a special order to feel in control. I just get bothered when things feel out of place, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific actions in response to certain thoughts or situations?\n\nPatient: Overwhelming urge… I don’t think so. Not in the way you’re asking, I guess.\n\nLike, if something’s bothering me, I might go deal with it because it’s sitting in my head—like if I remember I left something in the yard, I’ll go check, because one time I left a tarp out and it turned into this whole mess after it rained. But that’s not like I have to do some exact action or else something terrible is going to happen.\n\nI mean, I get annoyed and I want to fix things, especially around the house, but I wouldn’t call it an overwhelming urge. More like… I don’t like things hanging over me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

But that’s more about wanting things where I can find them, not like I’m following a ritual.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’m doing things in a special order to feel in control. I just get bothered when things feel out of place, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific actions in response to certain thoughts or situations?\n\nPatient: Overwhelming urge… I don’t think so. Not in the way you’re asking, I guess.\n\nLike, if something’s bothering me, I might go deal with it because it’s sitting in my head—like if I remember I left something in the yard, I’ll go check, because one time I left a tarp out and it turned into this whole mess after it rained. But that’s not like I have to do some exact action or else something terrible is going to happen.\n\nI mean, I get annoyed and I want to fix things, especially around the house, but I wouldn’t call it an overwhelming urge. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d say most of the day, no… I mean, I have my stretches where I’m low or kind of worn down, but “depressed most of the day” sounds heavier than how I’d put it.

Some days I’ll wake up already annoyed, like before anything’s even happened, and then I’ll go out to the garage thinking I’ll get something done, and three hours later I’ve mostly just moved things around. My neighbor once said retirement must be nice—though I’m not exactly retired—and I remember thinking, yeah, if only it felt like that.

But consistently down all day… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say that exactly. It comes and goes.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.0, F48.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I’m here because things have gotten kind of sideways at home. Ever since my father-in-law moved in, I’ve been snapping at my wife over stupid little house things — dishes, where stuff gets put, that kind of nonsense. And I don’t really like hearing myself do it.\n\nI used to keep on top of jobs around the place and paperwork, bills, forms, whatever, but lately I just keep putting it off. I’ll go out to the workshop thinking I’ll clear my head — I’ve got this old bench I’ve been meaning to fix, actually — or I’ll try to go fishing, because usually that settles me down. But my head just keeps running. It’s like I can’t get quiet inside.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d say every second of the day, but a lot of the time I feel wound up. Since my father-in-law moved in, it’s like I’m always bracing for something, and then I end up snapping at my wife over dumb little house stuff.\n\nEven when I try to go out to the workshop or take myself fishing — which usually helps, you know, just the quiet and doing something with my hands — my head doesn’t really settle. I keep thinking about the jobs I’m not doing, paperwork piling up, little things around the house. It just doesn’t get quiet in there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or hopeless lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… sad, maybe not exactly. Hopeless sounds a bit strong. I get fed up, I guess. Like when you go to fix one thing in the house and then notice six other things wrong — actually that happened with the shed door the other day, the hinge was sticking and I ended up pulling half the frame apart.\n\nBut, sorry… I’m not sure I’d call it sadness. More like I’m worn down and frustrated.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to do that you're avoiding now?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I suppose so. I mean, I still go out to the workshop sometimes, but I don’t really do what I went out there to do. I’ll stand around, move a few tools, then remember there’s paperwork inside I’ve been putting off, then somehow I’m looking at an old fishing reel that needs fixing… and nothing gets done.\n\nI used to stay on top of jobs around the house and bills and forms, that kind of thing. Since my father-in-law moved in, I just keep avoiding it. And even fishing — I still try, because usually that clears my head, but lately I can’t seem to get quiet inside, so it doesn’t feel the same. Mostly I end up snapping at my wife over little house stuff instead of actually dealing with anything.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain places, people, or activities because you fear something might happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s fear like that, no. I mean, I avoid some things, but it’s more because I don’t want to deal with them or I’m tired of the whole mess, not because I think something bad is going to happen.\n\nLike, I’ll put off going into town for errands, but then I’ll end up in the garage looking for something and find a box of old screws I forgot about—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. No, I haven’t noticed avoiding places or people because I’m scared something might happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you can't get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sort of. Not like a phrase over and over, exactly. It’s more that I can’t get my head to settle. Ever since my father-in-law moved in, it’s like everything in the house has become this thing I keep chewing on.\n\nI’ll be out in the workshop, meaning to fix something simple, and then I’m thinking about paperwork I haven’t done, or some little thing my wife said, or I end up snapping at her about, I don’t know, a cupboard door or something stupid. Even fishing doesn’t quiet it down like it used to. So yes, I guess the same worries keep circling.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing things over and over, like checking or cleaning, that you don't really want to be doing?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… not like checking the stove ten times or cleaning the same spot or anything like that.\n\nI mean, I’ll pick things up and put them down in the garage more than I need to, but that’s more me getting sidetracked than feeling like I have to do it. I don’t think there’s some routine I’m stuck repeating. Unless you mean something different by it?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest exactly. I still like the idea of things I used to enjoy, if that makes sense. Sometimes I’ll start something and then sort of drift off or get distracted.\n\nLike, I was going to sort through some fishing gear the other day, and then I ended up spending half an hour looking for a little plastic container I’m pretty sure I threw out two years ago. So… I don’t know. Maybe I’m not getting into things the same way, but I wouldn’t say everything feels completely uninteresting.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I haven’t been weighing myself or anything. My belt feels about the same, I guess.\n\nMy wife’s the one who’d probably notice before I would—she notices if I leave a coffee mug in the wrong place, so she’d definitely notice that. But no, nothing that’s jumped out at me as a big change.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced or witnessed any traumatic events recently?\n\nPatient: Not that I can think of, no. I mean, nothing like… I don’t know, an accident or violence or something like that.\n\nI did see a pretty bad wreck once years ago, but that’s not recent, and honestly I’m not sure why my brain went there. Lately it’s more just regular life stuff, I guess. Nothing I’d call traumatic recently.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're avoiding dealing with your father-in-law or the situation with him living with you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably am, in a way. Not like I’m hiding in the garage every time he walks in, but since he moved in, everything feels… crowded, I guess.\n\nI’ll put off dealing with stuff around the house, paperwork, little jobs I used to just knock out. And then I end up snapping at my wife over stupid things, like where something got put or how the kitchen is arranged. The other day I got irritated about a stack of mail like it was some major crisis, which is ridiculous.\n\nEven when I go out to the workshop or try to fish—things that usually settle me down—I can’t really get my head quiet. I start thinking about the house and him being there and what needs to be dealt with, and then I’m off on some whole other track. So… yeah, I guess I’m avoiding it some, or at least not handling it very well.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're emotionally distant from things or people that normally would affect you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I’d say distant exactly. I still care about my wife and what’s going on, and things do still get to me.\n\nSometimes I probably check out a little, like I’ll be sitting there and she’s talking and my mind wanders off to, I don’t know, whether the gutters need cleaning or something totally unrelated. My dad used to do that too—he’d stare right past you during dinner, and then suddenly ask if the lawn mower had gas in it. Drove my mom nuts.\n\nBut emotionally distant… I don’t know. Maybe not in the way you mean. I haven’t really noticed that I feel numb or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things you've said or done, especially towards your wife?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do feel bad about it, especially with my wife. I mean, I’m here because ever since my father-in-law moved in, I’ve been snapping at her over these tiny house things that shouldn’t matter that much.\n\nLike, I’ll get worked up about some job I haven’t done or paperwork sitting there, and instead of just dealing with it, I put it off and then somehow she catches the edge of it. It’s stupid. I used to be better at staying on top of that kind of stuff.\n\nAnd even when I try to get away from it—go out to the workshop or go fishing, which used to clear my head—I can’t really settle down. My mind just keeps chewing on the house situation, and then I come back already irritated. So yeah, I feel guilty. She doesn’t deserve me taking it out on her.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about the future or anticipating potential problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I do. It’s mostly tied up with the house, though. Ever since my father-in-law moved in, I’m always thinking ahead to what’s going to become a problem next.\n\nLike, there’s some paperwork sitting there, or a small repair I’d usually just handle, and instead I put it off and then it turns into this thing in my head. And then I’m short with my wife over something dumb, like where something got put.\n\nEven when I go out to the workshop or try to fish, my mind doesn’t really settle. I’ll be standing there with a rod in my hand thinking about some little house issue like it’s the end of the world. So yeah… I’m anticipating problems, probably more than I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your father-in-law moving in has significantly affected your emotional state?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it has. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like it’s all his fault or anything, because he’s just… he needs a place, you know. But since he moved in, I’ve felt more on edge.\n\nI’m here because I keep snapping at my wife over little house stuff, things that shouldn’t turn into arguments. And I’ve been putting off jobs and paperwork I used to just stay on top of. It’s like everything around the house feels bigger than it is.\n\nEven when I go out to the workshop or try to fish, which used to be my way of settling down, my head just won’t quiet. I’ll be thinking about some dumb chore or something I haven’t handled. So yeah… emotionally, it’s definitely affected me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat certain behaviors or rituals to feel okay or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like rituals, no.
